## Opinion & Explainers

### The Great Indian Bustard lost India's national bird race. It is now close to extinction (Opinion)

**In short:** Bharati Chaturvedi writes that the Great Indian Bustard (GIB), which lost the 1963 national bird contest to the Indian Peafowl, has lived in decline ever since, with naturalist Raol Shree Dharmakumarsinhji's estimate of about 1,260 GIBs across 11 states in the 1960s halved by the start of this century and under 150 GIBs existing today. Salim Ali had campaigned for the GIB, but the Ministry of Agriculture under the India Board of Wildlife rejected both the GIB and the Sarus Crane; the bird's Western Indian grassland habitat overlaps India's renewable energy route, creating a high-voltage clash where solar-energised wires at flight height electrocuted seven GIBs in 2022, following the 2019 Supreme Court case M.K.

- Bharati Chaturvedi writes that the Great Indian Bustard (GIB), which lost the 1963 national bird contest to the Indian Peafowl, has lived in decline ever since, with naturalist Raol Shree Dharmakumarsinhji's estimate of about 1,260 GIBs across 11 states in the 1960s halved by the start of this century and under 150 GIBs existing today.

### Lines on a map won't resolve India-China conflict (Opinion)

**In short:** Praveen Swami argues that speculation after the meeting between National Security Advisor Ajit Doval and Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi about an interim border agreement misses that the China-India border conflict has never been about the few dozen kilometres separating their claim-lines. He recounts how Beijing's 1962 use of force along the Line of Control was meant to send a message to New Delhi, Taiwan, the US and the Soviet Union amid PLA concerns over Xinjiang insurgency, CIA support to Tibetan and Kazakh rebels, and Indian asylum to secessionists including Yulbars Khan after 1951.

- Praveen Swami argues that speculation after the meeting between National Security Advisor Ajit Doval and Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi about an interim border agreement misses that the China-India border conflict has never been about the few dozen kilometres separating their claim-lines.
- He recounts how Beijing's 1962 use of force along the Line of Control was meant to send a message to New Delhi.
- Taiwan, the US and the Soviet Union amid PLA concerns over Xinjiang insurgency.
- CIA support to Tibetan and Kazakh rebels, and Indian asylum to secessionists including Yulbars Khan after 1951.
- The piece traces how British and Russian imperial missions.
- CIA officer Douglas Mackiernan's alliance with Kazakh rebel Usman Islamay, and the PLA-built Hotan-Rutog road in 1953 set the stage for China's Aksai Chin claims and the 1962 war.
_Sources: News Trail Mangaluru p.6_

### When childhood is no longer safe (Opinion)

**In short:** The piece cites chilling Delhi-NCR cases including a three-year-old girl allegedly sexually assaulted by a school bus driver in Bawana and a 16-year-old student allegedly gang-raped inside a moving sleeper bus after boarding at Greater Noida, arguing India is failing children and women in their basic right to live without fear. It says sexual violence is a national social emergency, calls for rigorous police verification, psychological screening and periodic re-verification of school transport staff, functioning CCTV, GPS and panic buttons on school buses, and suspension rather than merely fines for private buses with safety violations.

### What does Teachers' Day mean? (Opinion)

**In short:** The article questions whether Teachers' Day on September 5 truly recognises teachers or merely glorifies them for one day, noting teachers today are expected to be educators, mentors, counsellors, administrators, technology experts and more while also handling absentee follow-ups, government programmes, elections, census activities, surveys and awareness campaigns. It argues genuine respect means valuing teachers' time, professional expertise, intellectual freedom and right to focus on teaching and learning, and that teachers should not become responsible for every task simply because the system needs someone to perform it.

### The political cost of UCT schemes (Opinion)

**In short:** K.R. Shanmugam argues that unconditional cash transfer schemes such as Tamil Nadu's Kalaignar Magalir Urimai Thittam, despite costing States about $18 billion in 2025-26, can impose political costs through inclusion and exclusion errors—illustrated by the Tamil Nadu scheme covering 1.13 crore women and later adding 16.94 lakh more after complaints, at ₹13,807 crore in 2025-26—and that conditional transfers tied to education or other outcomes may carry lower political risk.

- K.R.

### RERA toothless (Opinion)

**In short:** N Sadhasiva Reddy of Bengaluru writes that the Real Estate (Regulation and Development) Act, promised as a game-changer for homebuyers, has become a toothless tiger because authorities issue refund and compensation orders that local revenue departments fail to execute, builders exploit insolvency proceedings under the IBC to freeze RERA orders, and buyers remain trapped between EMIs and legal fees without direct penal powers or strict enforcement.

### Juvenile justice faces Section 15 test (Opinion)

**In short:** Advocate Geeta Sajjanshetty and social worker Sangappa Vaggar argue the Supreme Court's adult-trial ruling for a 16-year-old accused of murder highlights Section 15's flawed design—assessing capacity presumes guilt, age correlates poorly with maturity, assessments vary widely without standardised protocols, and children's courts often ignore mandatory Section 19 reviews—calling on Parliament to devise a scientifically validated, rights-based framework instead of retribution.

- Advocate Geeta Sajjanshetty and social worker Sangappa Vaggar argue the Supreme Court's adult-trial ruling for a 16-year-old accused of murder highlights Section 15's flawed design—assessing capacity presumes guilt, age correlates poorly with maturity, assessments vary widely without standardised protocols, and children's courts often ignore mandatory Section 19 reviews—calling on Parliament to devise a scientifically validated, rights-based framework instead of retribution.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.8_

### A Ganesh Chaturthi without PoP pollution (Opinion)

**In short:** An editorial urges ecofriendly Ganesh Chaturthi celebrations, supporting Karnataka's push against Plaster of Paris and chemical-coloured idols and strict high court enforcement because PoP and toxic paints harm water bodies, while warning that loud DJ systems, route disputes and provocative processions—as seen in Nagamangala (2024) and Maddur (2025)—can turn festivity into law-and-order crises; organisers should follow police routes, sound limits and timings so faith and environmental and public peace coexist.

### A conversation is much more than words (Opinion)

**In short:** The writer recounts a family argument over a current event in which she spoke from feeling, her brother from reason and her sister from wider social implications, each using a different "language" of truth. She argues that before consequential discussions, people should recognise whether the exchange is emotional, logical or social, and respond in the language being spoken so empathy, coherence and perspective are not lost in translation.

### From Ambedkar to Rahul Gandhi—the unfinished fight against Manu (Opinion)

**In short:** The writer traces how B R Ambedkar's 1951 critique of Manusmriti, especially its subordination of women under male guardianship, fed into his battle for the Hindu Code Bill and resignation from the Cabinet when reform stalled before enactment in 1955–56. Rahul Gandhi's 2026 Pune call to "smash the patriarchy" echoes that unfinished struggle, but the piece argues it must move beyond slogans to concrete legal measures against digital sexual abuse, doxxing, deepfake pornography and threats targeting women in public life.

- The writer traces how B R Ambedkar's 1951 critique of Manusmriti, especially its subordination of women under male guardianship, fed into his battle for the Hindu Code Bill and resignation from the Cabinet when reform stalled before enactment in 1955–56.
- Rahul Gandhi's 2026 Pune call to "smash the patriarchy" echoes that unfinished struggle, but the piece argues it must move beyond slogans to concrete legal measures against digital sexual abuse, doxxing, deepfake pornography and threats targeting women in public life.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.9_

### Why you need a financial advisor even more now? (Opinion)

**In short:** Mrin Agarwal argues that exiting underperforming equity funds during weak phases often causes investors to miss subsequent recovery, creating a vicious cycle of chasing recent winners. Using the example of Anurag, who lacked adequate medical cover and held an illiquid, underperforming portfolio heavy in smallcap funds, she contends advisors deliver value through comprehensive planning, discipline during volatility, debt repayment strategy, international education savings, and tax filing support—not merely scheme selection.

- Mrin Agarwal argues that exiting underperforming equity funds during weak phases often causes investors to miss subsequent recovery, creating a vicious cycle of chasing recent winners.
- Using the example of Anurag, who lacked adequate medical cover and held an illiquid, underperforming portfolio heavy in smallcap funds, she contends advisors deliver value through comprehensive planning, discipline during volatility, debt repayment strategy, international education savings, and tax filing support—not merely scheme selection.
- She warns that panic selloffs, inadequate insurance, and poorly chosen equity schemes can cost 6–8% annually, making advisory fees the price of structure, objectivity, and fiduciary accountability.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.13_

### Does inflation targeting work in India? (Opinion)

**In short:** Rohit Azad and Indranil Chowdhury argue that after a decade of RBI inflation targeting aimed at 4% within a ±2 percentage-point band, India’s New Keynesian Phillips curve appears flat in monthly IIP and CPI data from April 2012 to March 2026, while household inflation expectations in RBI surveys consistently exceed projections by about four percentage points. They contend that with roughly 92% of workers lacking bargaining power, raising repo rates can lower output and employment without commensurate inflation gains—the opposite of what the framework assumes.

- Rohit Azad and Indranil Chowdhury argue that after a decade of RBI inflation targeting aimed at 4% within a ±2 percentage-point band.
- India’s New Keynesian Phillips curve appears flat in monthly IIP and CPI data from April 2012 to March 2026, while household inflation expectations in RBI surveys consistently exceed projections by about four percentage points.
- They contend that with roughly 92% of workers lacking bargaining power, raising repo rates can lower output and employment without commensurate inflation gains—the opposite of what the framework assumes.
_Sources: The Hindu Mangaluru p.17_
